\begin{theorem}\label{thm:main}
The equation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:main}
2x^{4}+xy+y^{3}+2y-1=0
\end{equation}
has no integer solutions.
\end{theorem}

The proof combines quadratic Hilbert symbols over $\Q$ and cubic Hilbert symbols over $K=\Q(\omega)$, where $\omega^2+\omega+1=0$. To a hypothetical integer solution we attach the values of auxiliary polynomials $H_i$ with rational coefficients, linked by a graph, and the values of auxiliary polynomials $J_i$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$, linked by a weighted graph. By the product formula and the reciprocity law, the products of the quadratic symbols over all places are $1$, and the sums of the cubic symbols over all places are $0$. We show that the local contributions vanish at the primes outside a finite set $S$, determine the sign contributions at the real place, compute the local contributions jointly at the primes of $S$, and obtain a contradiction. Apart from polynomial identities, which can be checked by expanding both sides, finite computations with residues, and basic properties of the Legendre symbol and of the quadratic Hilbert symbol, the proof uses two standard facts from class field theory: the explicit formula for the cubic Hilbert symbol at the places not above $3$, and the Hilbert reciprocity law.

\section{Legendre symbols and Hilbert symbols}

For a prime $p$ and a non-zero integer $N$, we write $v_p(N)$ for the exponent of $p$ in the prime factorization of $N$. For an integer $a$ and an odd prime $p$, the Legendre symbol $\leg{a}{p}$ is $0$ if $p\mid a$, $1$ if $a$ is a non-zero square modulo $p$, and $-1$ otherwise. It is multiplicative in $a$, and it depends only on $a$ modulo $p$ \cite[Chapter~5]{IR}.

For a prime $p$, let $\Q_p$ be the field of $p$-adic numbers, and put $\Q_\infty=\mathbb R$. For $v$ a prime or $v=\infty$, and non-zero $a,b\in\Q$, the Hilbert symbol $(a,b)_v$ is $1$ if the equation $z^2=ar^2+bs^2$ has a solution $(r,s,z)\neq(0,0,0)$ in $\Q_v$, and $-1$ otherwise. In particular, $(a,b)_v=1$ for every $v$ if this equation has a non-zero solution in $\Q$. For an odd integer $t$, we put $\varepsilon(t)=(t-1)/2$ and $\omega(t)=(t^2-1)/8$. We use the following facts \cite[Chapter~III, Proposition~2 and Theorems~1 and~3]{Se}.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:hilbert}
Let $a$, $a'$ and $b$ be non-zero integers, and let $v$ be a prime or $v=\infty$.
\begin{itemize}
\item[(i)] $(a,b)_\infty=-1$ if and only if $a<0$ and $b<0$.
\item[(ii)] Let $p$ be an odd prime, and write $a=p^\alpha u$ and $b=p^\beta w$ with $p\nmid uw$. Then $(a,b)_p=(-1)^{\alpha\beta\varepsilon(p)}\leg{u}{p}^\beta\leg{w}{p}^\alpha$.
\item[(iii)] Write $a=2^\alpha u$ and $b=2^\beta w$ with $u$ and $w$ odd. Then $(a,b)_2=(-1)^{\varepsilon(u)\varepsilon(w)+\alpha\omega(w)+\beta\omega(u)}$.
\item[(iv)] $(a,b)_v=1$ for all but finitely many $v$, and $\prod_v(a,b)_v=1$, the product over all primes $v$ and $v=\infty$.
\item[(v)] $(a,b)_v=(b,a)_v$ and $(aa',b)_v=(a,b)_v(a',b)_v$.
\end{itemize}
\end{theorem}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:square}
Let $a$ and $b$ be non-zero integers. If $p$ is an odd prime with $p\nmid a$ and $\leg{a}{p}=1$, then $(a,b)_p=1$. If $a\equiv1\pmod8$, then $(a,b)_2=1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The first claim is Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(ii) with $\alpha=0$. For the second, $\varepsilon(a)$ and $\omega(a)$ are even, and Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(iii) with $\alpha=0$ gives $(a,b)_2=1$.
\end{proof}


\begin{lemma}\label{lem:classes}
Let $p$ be a prime. For a non-zero integer $X$, write $X=p^ju$ with $p\nmid u$. If $p$ is odd, put $c_p(X)=(j,e)\in\F_2^2$, where $\leg{u}{p}=(-1)^e$; if $p=2$, put $c_2(X)=(j,e_1,e_2)\in\F_2^3$, where $u\equiv(-1)^{e_1}5^{e_2}\pmod8$; all entries are taken modulo $2$. Then $c_p(XX')=c_p(X)+c_p(X')$ and $(X,X')_p=(-1)^{\beta_p(c_p(X),c_p(X'))}$ for non-zero integers $X,X'$, where $\beta_p$ is the symmetric bilinear form
\begin{align*}
\beta_p\bigl((j,e),(j',e')\bigr)&=jj'\varepsilon(p)+je'+j'e&&\text{if $p$ is odd},\\
\beta_2\bigl((j,e_1,e_2),(j',e_1',e_2')\bigr)&=e_1e_1'+je_2'+j'e_2.
\end{align*}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For odd $p$, this is Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(ii), as the Legendre symbol is multiplicative. For $p=2$, the odd residues $1,3,5,7$ modulo $8$ are $(-1)^{e_1}5^{e_2}$ with $(e_1,e_2)=(0,0),(1,1),(0,1),(1,0)$, respectively; in each case $\varepsilon(u)\equiv e_1$ and $\omega(u)\equiv e_2\pmod2$, so Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(iii) gives the formula, and $c_2$ is additive because $(e_1,e_2)\mapsto(-1)^{e_1}5^{e_2}$ is an isomorphism from $\F_2^2$ to the group of odd residues modulo $8$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:residue}
Let $p$ be a prime, $k\geq1$, and let $X$ be a non-zero integer with $X\equiv r\pmod{p^k}$, where $0\leq r<p^k$. Then $c_p(X)\in b+D$ for the vector $b$ and the subspace $D$ given as follows. If $r=0$, then $b=0$ and $D$ is the whole space. Otherwise write $r=p^jr'$ with $p\nmid r'$, so that $j<k$. If $p$ is odd, or if $p=2$ and $k-j\geq3$, then $b=c_p(r)$ and $D=0$. If $p=2$ and $k-j=2$, then $b=(j,e_1,0)$ with $r'\equiv(-1)^{e_1}\pmod4$, and $D$ is spanned by $(0,0,1)$. If $p=2$ and $k-j=1$, then $b=(j,0,0)$, and $D$ is spanned by $(0,1,0)$ and $(0,0,1)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $r\neq0$, then $v_p(X)=j$ and $X/p^j\equiv r'\pmod{p^{k-j}}$. For odd $p$, the Legendre symbol of $X/p^j$ is therefore that of $r'$. For $p=2$, the residue of $X/2^j$ modulo $8$ is that of $r'$ if $k-j\geq3$, and its residue modulo $4$, which determines $e_1$, is that of $r'$ if $k-j=2$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:envelope}
Let $p$ be a prime, and let $E$ be a set of edges on a finite set of vertices. For every vertex $i$, let $b_i$ be a vector and $D_i$ a subspace of $\F_2^2$ (of $\F_2^3$ if $p=2$), and put $\sigma_i=\sum_{j\sim i}b_j$, the sum over the edges $\{i,j\}\in E$. Suppose that $\beta_p(d,\sigma_i)=0$ for every vertex $i$ and every $d\in D_i$, and that $\beta_p(d,d')=0$ for every edge $\{i,j\}\in E$ and all $d\in D_i$ and $d'\in D_j$. Then
\[
\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}\beta_p(c_i,c_j)=\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}\beta_p(b_i,b_j)\qquad\text{whenever $c_i\in b_i+D_i$ for every $i$.}
\]
By bilinearity, it suffices to check the hypotheses for $d$ and $d'$ in spanning sets of $D_i$ and $D_j$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $c_i=b_i+d_i$ with $d_i\in D_i$. As $\beta_p$ is bilinear and symmetric, the left side equals the right side plus $\sum_i\beta_p(d_i,\sigma_i)+\sum_{\{i,j\}\in E}\beta_p(d_i,d_j)$, and both sums vanish.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:amgm}
Let $a,b,c$ be real numbers with $a>0$, $c>0$ and $256ac^3>27b^4$. Then $at^4+bt+c>0$ for every real number $t$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $t=0$ this is clear. For $t\neq0$, the inequality between the arithmetic and geometric means of $at^4$, $c/3$, $c/3$ and $c/3$ gives $at^4+c\geq4\,(at^4c^3/27)^{1/4}=(256ac^3/27)^{1/4}|t|>|b|\,|t|\geq-bt$.
\end{proof}



\section{\texorpdfstring{Cubic Hilbert symbols over $\Q(\omega)$}{Cubic Hilbert symbols over Q(w)}}\label{sec:csymbols}

\paragraph{The field.} Let $K=\Q(\omega)$, where $\omega^2+\omega+1=0$. Its ring of integers is $\Z[\omega]$, and every element of $K$ can be written uniquely as $A+B\omega$ with $A,B\in\Q$. We write
\[
\langle A,B\rangle=A+B\omega,
\]
also when $A$ and $B$ are polynomials in $x$ and $y$. Then $\langle A,B\rangle\langle C,D\rangle=\langle AC-BD,\ AD+BC-BD\rangle$, and the norm is $N(\langle A,B\rangle)=A^2-AB+B^2$. In particular, a value $\langle A,B\rangle$ with $A,B\in\Z$ is zero if and only if $A=B=0$.

\paragraph{Places.} The finite places of $K$ correspond to the non-zero prime ideals of $\Z[\omega]$. A prime $p\equiv2\pmod3$ is inert: there is one place above $p$, with uniformizer $p$ and residue field $\Z[\omega]/p\Z[\omega]$ with $p^2$ elements. If $p\equiv1\pmod3$, the polynomial $t^2+t+1$ has two roots $r$ modulo $p$, and for each of them there is a place $v_r$ above $p$, at which $\omega$ is the root of $t^2+t+1$ in $\Z_p$ congruent to $r$ modulo $p$; the completion is $\Q_p$, the uniformizer is $p$, the residue field is $\F_p$, and $\omega$ reduces to $r$. The prime $3$ is ramified: $\pi=1-\omega$ generates the unique prime ideal above $3$, $\pi^2=-3\omega$, and $\Z[\omega]/\pi\Z[\omega]=\F_3$, where $\langle A,B\rangle$ reduces to $A+B$. The only infinite place of $K$ is complex. For a finite place $v$ we write $K_v$ for the completion and $v(a)$ for the normalized valuation, and we call $a$ a \emph{unit} at $v$ if $v(a)=0$.

\paragraph{Hilbert symbols.} For a place $v$ of $K$ and $a,b\in K_v^\times$, the cubic Hilbert symbol $(a,b)_v$ is a cube root of unity, see \cite[Chapter~V, \S3]{N}. We write $(a,b)_v=\omega^{\theta_v(a,b)}$ with $\theta_v(a,b)\in\Z/3\Z$, and we use the following three facts \cite[Chapter~V, \S3 and Chapter~VI, \S8]{N}.
\begin{itemize}
\item[(H1)] $\theta_v(a,bc)=\theta_v(a,b)+\theta_v(a,c)$, $\theta_v(a,b)=-\theta_v(b,a)$, and $\theta_v(a,c^3)=0$. In particular, $\theta_v(a,b)$ depends only on the classes of $a$ and $b$ in $K_v^\times/K_v^{\times3}$.
\item[(H2)] Let $v$ be a finite place not above $3$, let $q$ be the number of elements of its residue field, and let $\varpi$ be a uniformizer at $v$. Write $a=\varpi^eu$ and $b=\varpi^fw$ with units $u$ and $w$, and define $\chi(u)\in\Z/3\Z$ by $\bar u^{(q-1)/3}=\bar\omega^{\chi(u)}$, where the bar denotes the reduction to the residue field. Then $\theta_v(a,b)=e\,\chi(w)-f\,\chi(u)$. In particular, $\theta_v(a,b)=0$ if $a$ and $b$ are both units.
\item[(H3)] (Hilbert reciprocity.) For $a,b\in K^\times$, $\theta_v(a,b)=0$ for all but finitely many places $v$, and $\sum_v\theta_v(a,b)=0$, where the sum is over all places of $K$. At the complex place, $\theta_v(a,b)=0$.
\end{itemize}
The general formula in (H2) contains an additional term $ef\chi(-1)$, which vanishes because $-1=(-1)^3$ is a cube. Formula (H2) fixes the normalization of the symbols; with the opposite sign convention all values $\theta_v$ change sign, and the proof below is the same.

\paragraph{Classes at the places not above $3$.} Let $p\neq3$ be a prime and $v$ a place above $p$. For $a=p^eu\in K_v^\times$ with a unit $u$, put $\mathrm{cl}_v(a)=(e\bmod3,\ \chi(u))\in\F_3^2$. By (H2) and (H1), $\mathrm{cl}_v$ is additive, $\mathrm{cl}_v(a)$ determines the class of $a$ modulo cubes, and $\theta_v(a,b)=e\chi(w)-f\chi(u)$ for $\mathrm{cl}_v(a)=(e,\chi(u))$ and $\mathrm{cl}_v(b)=(f,\chi(w))$. We put $\mathrm{cl}_p(a)=\mathrm{cl}_v(a)\in\F_3^2$ if $p\equiv2\pmod3$, and $\mathrm{cl}_p(a)=(\mathrm{cl}_{v_r}(a),\mathrm{cl}_{v_{r'}}(a))\in\F_3^4$ if $p\equiv1\pmod3$, where $r<r'$ are the roots of $t^2+t+1$ in $\{1,\dots,p-1\}$; accordingly, $\theta_p$ is the sum of the forms $\theta_v$ over the places $v$ above $p$. For $p\equiv2\pmod3$ and a rational integer $u$ prime to $p$, we have $\bar u\in\F_p$, so that $\bar u^{(p^2-1)/3}=(\bar u^{p-1})^{(p+1)/3}=1$ and $\chi(u)=0$.

\paragraph{The place above $3$.}
\begin{lemma}\label{lem:cM3}
Let $v$ be the place above $3$. Every non-zero element of $K_v$ is equal to $\pi^a\omega^b2^c(1+3\omega)^d$ times a cube, for unique $a,b,c,d\in\Z/3\Z$, and every unit congruent to $1$ modulo $9$ is a cube. Put $\mathrm{cl}_3(A)=(a,b,c,d)\in\F_3^4$. Then $\mathrm{cl}_3$ is additive, and $\theta_v(A,B)=\mathrm{cl}_3(A)^{\mathsf T}M_3\,\mathrm{cl}_3(B)$, where
\[
M_3=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&2&0\\0&0&1&2\\1&2&0&0\\0&1&0&0\end{pmatrix}\pmod3.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $\alpha\in\Z[\omega]$, the polynomial $g(s)=s+3s^2+3s^3-\alpha$ satisfies $g'(s)\equiv1\pmod\pi$, so by Hensel's lemma it has a root $s$ in the completion of $\Z[\omega]$, and then $(1+3s)^3=1+9(s+3s^2+3s^3)=1+9\alpha$. Hence every unit congruent to $1$ modulo $9$ is a cube in $K_v$, and the class of a unit modulo cubes depends only on its residue modulo $9$. There are $54$ units in $\Z[\omega]/(9)$, and a direct enumeration shows that the $27$ products $\omega^b2^c(1+3\omega)^d$ with $b,c,d\in\{0,1,2\}$ are pairwise distinct modulo $9$, also after multiplication by $-1=(-1)^3$. Hence they represent all $27$ classes of units modulo cubes. Since $\pi^3$ is a cube times a unit, the exponent of $\pi$ is the valuation modulo $3$, and the first statement follows.

By (H1), it remains to compute $\theta_v$ on the $16$ ordered pairs of the elements $\pi$, $\omega$, $2$ and $1+3\omega$. Their norms are $3$, $1$, $4$ and $7$, hence, for each such pair, all the symbols at the places not above $2$, $3$ and $7$ vanish by (H2), and, by (H3), $\theta_v$ at the place above $3$ is minus the sum of the values at the place above $2$ and at the two places above $7$. These places correspond to $\bar\omega=2$ and $\bar\omega=4$ in $\F_7$. The classes $(e,\chi)$ of the four elements at these places are
\[
\begin{array}{c|ccc}
 & 2 & \bar\omega=2 & \bar\omega=4\\\hline
\pi & (0,2) & (0,0) & (0,2)\\
\omega & (0,1) & (0,2) & (0,2)\\
2 & (1,0) & (0,2) & (0,1)\\
1+3\omega & (0,2) & (1,0) & (0,0)
\end{array}
\]
where, at the places above $7$, $\chi(u)$ is defined by $\bar u^2=\bar\omega^{\chi(u)}$ in $\F_7$, and $7$ is a uniformizer. Formula (H2) and the sum over these three places give $M_3$.
\end{proof}

We write $\theta_3$ for the form of Lemma~\ref{lem:cM3}. Since $3=-\omega^2\pi^2$ and $4=2^2$, we have $\mathrm{cl}_3(3)=(2,2,0,0)$ and $\mathrm{cl}_3(4)=(0,0,2,0)$.

\paragraph{Residue classes.} The local computations use the following description of the classes of the values of a polynomial on a residue class.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:cresidue}
Let $p$ be a prime, $k\geq1$, and let $z\in\Z[\omega]$ be non-zero with $z\equiv\zeta\pmod{p^k}$, where $\zeta=\langle A,B\rangle$ with $0\leq A,B<p^k$. Then $\mathrm{cl}_p(z)\in b+D$ for the vector $b$ and the subspace $D$ given as follows.
\begin{itemize}
\item[(i)] $p\equiv2\pmod3$. If $\zeta\neq0$, then $b=\mathrm{cl}_p(\zeta)$ and $D=0$. If $\zeta=0$, then $b=0$, and $D$ is $\F_3^2$, or the span of $(1,0)$ if $z\in\Z$.
\item[(ii)] $p\equiv1\pmod3$. For each root $r$, let $\rho$ be the root of $t^2+t+1$ modulo $p^k$ congruent to $r$. If $A+B\rho\not\equiv0\pmod{p^k}$, the two coordinates of $\mathrm{cl}_{v_r}(z)$ are those of $\mathrm{cl}_{v_r}(\zeta)$; otherwise these two coordinates are arbitrary.
\item[(iii)] $p=3$, $z\notin\Z$. If $\zeta\neq0$, let $a=v(\zeta)<2k$ be its valuation at $\pi$ and $m=2k-a$. Then $b=\mathrm{cl}_3(\zeta)$, and $D=0$ if $m\geq4$, while $D=D_m$ if $m\leq3$, where $D_1$ is spanned by $(0,1,0,0)$, $(0,0,1,0)$ and $(0,0,0,1)$, $D_2$ by $(0,0,1,0)$ and $(0,0,0,1)$, and $D_3$ by $(0,0,1,1)$. If $\zeta=0$, then $b=0$ and $D=\F_3^4$.
\item[(iv)] $p=3$, $z\in\Z$, so that $B=0$. If $3^k\nmid A$, let $j=v_3(A)$. Then $b=\mathrm{cl}_3(A)$ and $D=0$ if $k-j\geq2$, while $b=j\,\mathrm{cl}_3(3)$ and $D$ is spanned by $\mathrm{cl}_3(4)$ if $k-j=1$. If $3^k\mid A$, then $b=0$ and $D$ is spanned by $\mathrm{cl}_3(3)$ and $\mathrm{cl}_3(4)$.
\end{itemize}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(i) If $\zeta\neq0$, then $e=v(z)=v(\zeta)<k$ and $z/p^e\equiv\zeta/p^e\pmod p$, so that $\chi$ is determined. If $z\in\Z$, its unit part is a rational integer, and $\chi=0$. (ii) At $v_r$, $z$ and $\zeta$ are the $p$-adic numbers $A'+B'\omega$ and $A+B\omega$ with $\omega\equiv\rho\pmod{p^k}$, and the argument of (i) applies. (iii) If $\zeta\neq0$, then $v(z)=a$, and $z/\pi^a\equiv\zeta/\pi^a\pmod{\pi^m}$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:cM3}, the class of a unit depends only on its residue modulo $9=\omega\pi^4$; the units congruent to $1$ modulo $\pi^m$ form a subgroup, and a direct enumeration of their residues modulo $9$ shows that their classes form $D_m$. (iv) If $3^k\nmid A$, then $z=3^ju$ with a rational unit $u\equiv A/3^j\pmod{3^{k-j}}$. The class of a rational unit depends only on its residue modulo $9$, the units $\pm1$ are cubes, and the rational units congruent to $1$ modulo $3$ are $4^s$ times a unit congruent to $1$ modulo $9$; hence the classes are as stated, and if $3^k\mid A$ the valuation $j$ is also unknown.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:cenvelope}
Let $p$ be a prime, and consider vertices $i$ with vectors $b_i$, subspaces $D_i$ and vectors $\gamma_i$, and a set of edges $(i,j)$ with $i<j$ and weights $w_{ij}\in\{1,2\}$. Put $\rho_{ij}=w_{ij}$ and $\rho_{ji}=-w_{ij}$ for the edges, and $\sigma_i=\gamma_i+\sum_j\rho_{ij}b_j$. Suppose that $\theta_p(d,\sigma_i)=0$ for every $i$ and every $d\in D_i$, and that $\theta_p(d,d')=0$ for every edge $(i,j)$ and all $d\in D_i$, $d'\in D_j$. Then
\[
\sum_{(i,j)}w_{ij}\theta_p(c_i,c_j)+\sum_i\theta_p(c_i,\gamma_i)=\sum_{(i,j)}w_{ij}\theta_p(b_i,b_j)+\sum_i\theta_p(b_i,\gamma_i)
\]
whenever $c_i\in b_i+D_i$ for every $i$. By bilinearity, it suffices to check the hypotheses for $d$ and $d'$ in spanning sets of $D_i$ and $D_j$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $c_i=b_i+d_i$ with $d_i\in D_i$. As $\theta_p$ is bilinear and $\theta_p(u,v)=-\theta_p(v,u)$, the left side equals the right side plus $\sum_i\theta_p(d_i,\sigma_i)+\sum_{(i,j)}w_{ij}\theta_p(d_i,d_j)$, and both sums vanish.
\end{proof}

\section{The quadratic certificate}

Put $F(x,y)=2x^{4}+xy+y^{3}+2y-1$, so that \eqref{eq:main} is the equation $F(x,y)=0$. The vertices are the polynomials
\begin{align*}
H_{0}&=-y,\\
H_{1}&=3x^{2}-3xy+y^{2}+y+1,\\
H_{2}&=x^{2}+3x+3,\\
H_{3}&=x^{2}-xy+x+y^{2}-2y+1,\\
H_{4}&=x^{2}+xy+2x+y^{2}+y+1,\\
H_{5}&=x^{2}-xy-x+y^{2}-y+1,\\
H_{6}&=x^{2}+xy-x+y^{2}-2y+1,\\
H_{7}&=-y+1,\\
H_{8}&=3x^{2}+3xy+y^{2}+y+1,\\
H_{9}&=x^{2}-x+1,\\
H_{10}&=x^{2}+xy-2x+y^{2}-y+1,\\
H_{11}&=x^{2}+2xy-x+y^{2}-y+1,\\
H_{12}&=-2y+1,\\
H_{13}&=x^{2}+xy+y^{2},\\
H_{14}&=x^{2}-xy+y^{2},\\
H_{15}&=2xy+4x,\\
H_{16}&=-x-1,\\
H_{17}&=-x+1,\\
H_{18}&=-x^{2}-2x+y+1,\\
H_{19}&=-1,\\
H_{20}&=-x^{2}-xy-x+y^{2}+y,\\
H_{21}&=-x^{2}+2xy-3x-y^{2}+y,\\
H_{22}&=y-1,\\
H_{23}&=-x^{2}+2xy-2x-y^{2}+y+1,\\
H_{24}&=x^{2}+xy-x+2y,\\
H_{25}&=x^{2}-xy-2x-y^{2}-y+1,\\
H_{26}&=x-1,\\
H_{27}&=y,\\
H_{28}&=x^{2}+xy-3x+y^{2},\\
H_{29}&=x^{2}+2xy-3x+y^{2}-y,\\
H_{30}&=2x-1,\\
H_{31}&=x^{2}+3x+2y,\\
H_{32}&=x^{2}+xy+x+y^{2}-y+1,\\
H_{33}&=x^{4}-x^{2}y+y^{2},\\
H_{34}&=x^{3}-x^{2}y+y^{2}+y,\\
H_{35}&=x^{2}-xy+x+y^{2}+y+1,\\
H_{36}&=-y-1,\\
H_{37}&=x^{3}+x^{2}+y^{2},\\
H_{38}&=x^{3}-x^{2}+y^{2},\\
H_{39}&=x^{3}-x^{2}y+x^{2}+y^{2}+y,\\
H_{40}&=x^{2}-xy+3x+y^{2}+3,\\
H_{41}&=-x^{3}-x^{2}y+xy+y,\\
H_{42}&=-x^{3}-x^{2}y+y,\\

\section{The cubic certificate}

The certificate consists of 40 polynomials $J_i$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$ and constants $c_i\in\Z[\omega]$, listed in Table~\ref{tab:cvert1}, and of 163 \emph{edges} $(i,j)$ with $i<j$ and weights $w_{ij}\in\{1,2\}$, listed below. For an edge $(i,j)$ we put $\rho_{ij}=w_{ij}$ and $\rho_{ji}=-w_{ij}\bmod 3$, and $\rho_{ij}=0$ if $i$ and $j$ are not joined by an edge.
The edges $(i,j;w_{ij})$ are $(0,4;1)$, $(0,5;2)$, $(0,14;1)$, $(0,21;1)$, $(0,24;1)$, $(0,34;1)$, $(1,3;1)$, $(1,5;1)$, $(1,11;2)$, $(1,12;1)$, $(1,13;2)$, $(1,15;1)$, $(1,22;1)$, $(1,38;2)$, $(23,37;1)$, $(23,38;2)$, $(25,31;1)$, $(26,28;2)$, $(26,29;2)$, $(26,31;2)$, $(26,33;1)$, $(26,37;2)$, $(27,28;1)$, $(27,39;1)$, $(28,29;1)$, $(28,31;1)$, $(28,34;2)$, $(28,38;1)$, $(29,31;2)$, $(29,35;1)$, $(29,39;2)$, $(30,38;2)$, $(31,32;1)$, $(31,34;2)$, $(31,37;2)$, $(31,38;1)$, $(34,39;2)$.
Let $(\xi,\eta)$ denote $(x,y)$ or $(y,x)$. We call a vertex $i$ \emph{linear} if $J_i=\varepsilon_i(\eta-r_i(\xi))$ for a unit $\varepsilon_i$ of $\Z[\omega]$ and a polynomial $r_i$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$; the last column of Table~\ref{tab:cvert1} gives $\eta=r_i(\xi)$ for the linear vertices. For a linear vertex $i$, we put $f_i(t)=F$ and $g_{ij}(t)=J_j$ evaluated at $\xi=t$, $\eta=r_i(t)$; these are polynomials in $t$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$.
For every linear vertex $i$, Table~\ref{tab:cstar} gives an integer $D_i\geq1$ and a polynomial $w_i(t)$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cstarlin}
D_i^3\,c_i\prod_jg_{ij}^{\rho_{ij}}-w_i^3=f_iQ_i
\end{equation}
for a polynomial $Q_i(t)$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$, where $\rho_{ij}\in\{0,1,2\}$; $Q_i$ is the quotient of the division of the left side by $f_i$, and the remainder is zero.
For every other vertex $i$, Table~\ref{tab:cstar} gives an integer $D_i\geq1$ and polynomials $T_i$, $U_i$ and $V_i$ in $x$ and $y$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cstar}
D_i^3\,c_i\prod_jJ_j^{\rho_{ij}}-T_i^3=U_iF+V_iJ_i.
\end{equation}
For every edge $(i,j)$ with a linear endpoint, Table~\ref{tab:csep} gives the first linear endpoint $k\in\{i,j\}$ and the factorization of the norm of the resultant $R_{ij}=\operatorname{Res}_t(f_k,g_{kl})$, where $\{k,l\}=\{i,j\}$; it is non-zero.
For the other edges, Table~\ref{tab:cbez} gives a non-zero constant $D_{ij}\in\Z[\omega]$ and polynomials $\lambda_{ij}$, $\mu_{ij}$ and $\nu_{ij}$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:csep}
\lambda_{ij}F+\mu_{ij}J_i+\nu_{ij}J_j=D_{ij}.
\end{equation}
These identities can be checked by expanding both sides and by polynomial division, using $\omega^2=-1-\omega$.

\section{Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:main}}

Suppose that $(x,y)\in\Z^2$ is a solution of \eqref{eq:main}, so that $F(x,y)=0$; from now on the polynomials denote their values at $(x,y)$.
\paragraph{Step 1: the values are non-zero.} For $i\in\{16,\allowbreak 17,\allowbreak 23,\allowbreak 25,\allowbreak 26,\allowbreak 30,\allowbreak 33,\allowbreak 37,\allowbreak 38,\allowbreak 41,\allowbreak 42,\allowbreak 43,\allowbreak 44,\allowbreak 46,\allowbreak 48,\allowbreak 52,\allowbreak 55,\allowbreak 57,\allowbreak 60,\allowbreak 63,\allowbreak 64,\allowbreak 65,\allowbreak 67,\allowbreak 68,\allowbreak 69,\allowbreak 72,\allowbreak 79,\allowbreak 81,\allowbreak 93,\allowbreak 96\}$, checking the $2^2$ residue pairs modulo $2$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $2$, so $H_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{29,\allowbreak 49,\allowbreak 56,\allowbreak 71\}$, checking the $3^2$ residue pairs modulo $3$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $3$, so $H_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{21,\allowbreak 24,\allowbreak 47,\allowbreak 53,\allowbreak 77,\allowbreak 100\}$, checking the $4^2$ residue pairs modulo $4$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $4$, so $H_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{15,\allowbreak 34,\allowbreak 92,\allowbreak 103\}$, checking the $7^2$ residue pairs modulo $7$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $7$, so $H_i\neq0$. Checking the $8^2$ residue pairs modulo $8$, we find that $F$ and $H_{82}$ have no common zero modulo $8$, so $H_{82}\neq0$. For $i\in\{20,\allowbreak 45,\allowbreak 61,\allowbreak 80\}$, checking the $11^2$ residue pairs modulo $11$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $11$, so $H_i\neq0$. Checking the $13^2$ residue pairs modulo $13$, we find that $F$ and $H_{39}$ have no common zero modulo $13$, so $H_{39}\neq0$. For $i\in\{51,\allowbreak 59\}$, checking the $19^2$ residue pairs modulo $19$, we find that $F$ and $H_i$ have no common zero modulo $19$, so $H_i\neq0$. Checking the $23^2$ residue pairs modulo $23$, we find that $F$ and $H_{98}$ have no common zero modulo $23$, so $H_{98}\neq0$. We have $H_{85}=-x(x+y)$, and checking residue pairs, we find that $F$ and $x$ have no common zero modulo $3$ and $F$ and $x+y$ have no common zero modulo $2$; hence $H_{85}\neq0$. The other non-constant $H_i$ are non-zero by Step~2. For $i\in\{0,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 2,\allowbreak 3,\allowbreak 4,\allowbreak 5,\allowbreak 7,\allowbreak 8,\allowbreak 10,\allowbreak 11,\allowbreak 12,\allowbreak 13,\allowbreak 14,\allowbreak 15,\allowbreak 16,\allowbreak 17,\allowbreak 18,\allowbreak 19,\allowbreak 20,\allowbreak 22,\allowbreak 24,\allowbreak 25,\allowbreak 30,\allowbreak 31,\allowbreak 32,\allowbreak 33,\allowbreak 34,\allowbreak 35\}$, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $2$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{2}$, as one checks for the $2^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{6,\allowbreak 9,\allowbreak 21,\allowbreak 23,\allowbreak 27,\allowbreak 37,\allowbreak 38\}$, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $3$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{3}$, as one checks for the $3^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{28,\allowbreak 29,\allowbreak 36\}$, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $4$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{4}$, as one checks for the $4^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. For $i\in\{26,\allowbreak 39\}$, there is no residue pair $(a,b)$ modulo $5$ with $F(a,b)\equiv0$ and $J_i(a,b)\equiv0\pmod{5}$, as one checks for the $5^2$ residue pairs; hence $J_i\neq0$. Also, all $c_i$ are non-zero.

\paragraph{Step 2: signs.} \emph{The sign of $H_{0}$.} Suppose that $H_{0}\leq0$. Since $H_{0}=-y$, this means $y\geq0$. By \eqref{eq:sign0}, $F$ is $2x^4+L_{0}x+Q_{0}$, where $L_{0}$ and $Q_{0}$ depend only on $y$. If $y\geq1$, write $y=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; expanding $Q_{0}$ and $\Delta_{0}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $Q_{0}>0$ and $\Delta_{0}>0$. For $y=0$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $x$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $y$. Hence $Q_{0}>0$ and $\Delta_{0}>0$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:amgm} with $a=2$, $b=L_{0}$, $c=Q_{0}$ and $t=x$ gives $F(x,y)>0$, a contradiction. Hence $H_{0}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{1}$.} If $H_{1}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign1} gives $(3x-2y-1)^2\leq 0$ and $3(x+1)^2\leq 0$, so $|3x-2y-1|\leq0$ and $|x+1|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(-1,-2)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{1}\leq0$. Hence $H_{1}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{2}$.} By \eqref{eq:sign2}, $H_{2}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{3}$.} If $H_{3}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign3} gives $(x-2y+2)^2\leq 0$ and $3x^2\leq 0$, so $|x-2y+2|\leq0$ and $|x|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(0,1)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{3}\leq0$. Hence $H_{3}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{4}$.} If $H_{4}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign4} gives $(x+2y+1)^2\leq 0$ and $3(x+1)^2\leq 0$, so $|x+2y+1|\leq0$ and $|x+1|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(-1,0)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{4}\leq0$. Hence $H_{4}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{5}$.} If $H_{5}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign5} gives $(x-2y+1)^2\leq 0$ and $3(x-1)^2\leq 0$, so $|x-2y+1|\leq0$ and $|x-1|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(1,1)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{5}\leq0$. Hence $H_{5}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{6}$.} If $H_{6}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign6} gives $(x+2y-2)^2\leq 0$ and $3x^2\leq 0$, so $|x+2y-2|\leq0$ and $|x|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(0,1)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{6}\leq0$. Hence $H_{6}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{7}$.} Suppose that $H_{7}\leq0$. Since $H_{7}=-y+1$, this means $y\geq1$. By \eqref{eq:sign7}, $F$ is $2x^4+L_{7}x+Q_{7}$, where $L_{7}$ and $Q_{7}$ depend only on $y$. If $y\geq1$, write $y=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; expanding $Q_{7}$ and $\Delta_{7}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $Q_{7}>0$ and $\Delta_{7}>0$. Hence $Q_{7}>0$ and $\Delta_{7}>0$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:amgm} with $a=2$, $b=L_{7}$, $c=Q_{7}$ and $t=x$ gives $F(x,y)>0$, a contradiction. Hence $H_{7}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{8}$.} If $H_{8}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign8} gives $(3x+2y+1)^2\leq 0$ and $3(x-1)^2\leq 0$, so $|3x+2y+1|\leq0$ and $|x-1|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(1,-2)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{8}\leq0$. Hence $H_{8}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{9}$.} By \eqref{eq:sign9}, $H_{9}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{10}$.} If $H_{10}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign10} gives $(x+2y-1)^2\leq 0$ and $3(x-1)^2\leq 0$, so $|x+2y-1|\leq0$ and $|x-1|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(1,0)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{10}\leq0$. Hence $H_{10}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{11}$.} By \eqref{eq:sign11}, $H_{11}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{12}$.} Suppose that $H_{12}\leq0$. Since $H_{12}=-2y+1$, this means $y\geq1$. By \eqref{eq:sign12}, $F$ is $2x^4+L_{12}x+Q_{12}$, where $L_{12}$ and $Q_{12}$ depend only on $y$. If $y\geq1$, write $y=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; expanding $Q_{12}$ and $\Delta_{12}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $Q_{12}>0$ and $\Delta_{12}>0$. Hence $Q_{12}>0$ and $\Delta_{12}>0$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:amgm} with $a=2$, $b=L_{12}$, $c=Q_{12}$ and $t=x$ gives $F(x,y)>0$, a contradiction. Hence $H_{12}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{13}$.} If $H_{13}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign13} gives $(x+2y)^2\leq 0$ and $3x^2\leq 0$, so $|x+2y|\leq0$ and $|x|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(0,0)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{13}\leq0$. Hence $H_{13}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{14}$.} If $H_{14}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign14} gives $(x-2y)^2\leq 0$ and $3x^2\leq 0$, so $|x-2y|\leq0$ and $|x|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(0,0)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{14}\leq0$. Hence $H_{14}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{18}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign18} gives $Q_{18}(-H_{18})=M_{18}$, and $M_{18}$ and $E_{18}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{18}>0$ and $E_{18}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-3$, write $x=-3-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{18}$ and $E_{18}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{18}>0$ and $E_{18}>0$. It remains to consider $-2\leq x\leq0$. For $x=-2$ we have $M_{18}>0$ and $E_{18}>0$. For $x=-1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 2 residues of $y$ modulo $2$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=0$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{3}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 3 residues of $y$ modulo $3$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{18}>0$ and $E_{18}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign18b} gives $4Q_{18}\geq E_{18}>0$, and $Q_{18}(-H_{18})=M_{18}>0$ gives $(-H_{18})>0$. Hence $H_{18}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{22}$.} Suppose that $(-H_{22})\leq0$. Since $(-H_{22})=-y+1$, this means $y\geq1$. By \eqref{eq:sign22}, $F$ is $2x^4+L_{22}x+Q_{22}$, where $L_{22}$ and $Q_{22}$ depend only on $y$. If $y\geq1$, write $y=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; expanding $Q_{22}$ and $\Delta_{22}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $Q_{22}>0$ and $\Delta_{22}>0$. Hence $Q_{22}>0$ and $\Delta_{22}>0$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:amgm} with $a=2$, $b=L_{22}$, $c=Q_{22}$ and $t=x$ gives $F(x,y)>0$, a contradiction. Hence $(-H_{22})>0$. Hence $H_{22}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{27}$.} Suppose that $(-H_{27})\leq0$. Since $(-H_{27})=-y$, this means $y\geq0$. By \eqref{eq:sign27}, $F$ is $2x^4+L_{27}x+Q_{27}$, where $L_{27}$ and $Q_{27}$ depend only on $y$. If $y\geq1$, write $y=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; expanding $Q_{27}$ and $\Delta_{27}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $Q_{27}>0$ and $\Delta_{27}>0$. For $y=0$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $x$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $y$. Hence $Q_{27}>0$ and $\Delta_{27}>0$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:amgm} with $a=2$, $b=L_{27}$, $c=Q_{27}$ and $t=x$ gives $F(x,y)>0$, a contradiction. Hence $(-H_{27})>0$. Hence $H_{27}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{28}$.} If $H_{28}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign28} gives $(x+2y)^2\leq 12$ and $3(x-2)^2\leq 12$, so $|x+2y|\leq3$ and $|x-2|\leq2$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer points satisfying both inequalities are $(0,-1)$, $(0,0)$, $(0,1)$, $(1,-2)$, $(1,-1)$, $(1,0)$, $(1,1)$, $(2,-2)$, $(2,-1)$, $(2,0)$, $(3,-3)$, $(3,-2)$, $(3,-1)$, $(3,0)$, $(4,-3)$, $(4,-2)$, $(4,-1)$, and none of them is a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{28}\leq0$. Hence $H_{28}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{31}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign31} gives $Q_{31}H_{31}=M_{31}$, and $M_{31}$ and $E_{31}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{31}>0$ and $E_{31}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-9$, write $x=-9-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{31}$ and $E_{31}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{31}>0$ and $E_{31}>0$. It remains to consider $-8\leq x\leq0$. For $x=0$ we have $M_{31}>0$ and $E_{31}>0$. For $x\in\{-7,\allowbreak -5,\allowbreak -3,\allowbreak -1\}$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 2 residues of $y$ modulo $2$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with such $x$. For $x=-6$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{3}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 3 residues of $y$ modulo $3$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=-8$ and $x=-2$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{7}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 7 residues of $y$ modulo $7$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with such $x$. For $x=-4$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{23}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 23 residues of $y$ modulo $23$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{31}>0$ and $E_{31}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign31b} gives $16Q_{31}\geq E_{31}>0$, and $Q_{31}H_{31}=M_{31}>0$ gives $H_{31}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{32}$.} If $H_{32}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign32} gives $(x+2y-1)^2\leq 0$ and $3(x+1)^2\leq 0$, so $|x+2y-1|\leq0$ and $|x+1|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(-1,1)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{32}\leq0$. Hence $H_{32}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{33}$.} By \eqref{eq:sign33}, $H_{33}\geq0$, and $H_{33}\neq0$ by Step~1, so $H_{33}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{35}$.} If $H_{35}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign35} gives $(x-2y-1)^2\leq 0$ and $3(x+1)^2\leq 0$, so $|x-2y-1|\leq0$ and $|x+1|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(-1,-1)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{35}\leq0$. Hence $H_{35}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{36}$.} Suppose that $H_{36}\leq0$. Since $H_{36}=-y-1$, this means $y\geq-1$. By \eqref{eq:sign36}, $F$ is $2x^4+L_{36}x+Q_{36}$, where $L_{36}$ and $Q_{36}$ depend only on $y$. If $y\geq1$, write $y=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; expanding $Q_{36}$ and $\Delta_{36}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $Q_{36}>0$ and $\Delta_{36}>0$. For $y=-1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{11}$ for every $x$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $y$. For $y=0$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $x$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $y$. Hence $Q_{36}>0$ and $\Delta_{36}>0$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:amgm} with $a=2$, $b=L_{36}$, $c=Q_{36}$ and $t=x$ gives $F(x,y)>0$, a contradiction. Hence $H_{36}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{40}$.} If $H_{40}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign40} gives $(x-2y)^2\leq 0$ and $3(x+2)^2\leq 0$, so $|x-2y|\leq0$ and $|x+2|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(-2,-1)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{40}\leq0$. Hence $H_{40}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{43}$.} By \eqref{eq:sign43}, $H_{43}\geq0$, and $H_{43}\neq0$ by Step~1, so $H_{43}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{50}$.} By \eqref{eq:sign50}, $H_{50}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{54}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign54} gives $Q_{54}H_{54}=M_{54}$, and $M_{54}$ and $E_{54}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{54}>0$ and $E_{54}>0$. If $x\geq5$, write $x=5+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-1$, write $x=-1-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{54}$ and $E_{54}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{54}>0$ and $E_{54}>0$. It remains to consider $0\leq x\leq4$. For $x=1$ and $x=3$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 2 residues of $y$ modulo $2$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with such $x$. For $x=0$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{3}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 3 residues of $y$ modulo $3$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=4$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{5}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 5 residues of $y$ modulo $5$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=2$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{29}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 29 residues of $y$ modulo $29$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{54}>0$ and $E_{54}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign54b} gives $4Q_{54}\geq E_{54}>0$, and $Q_{54}H_{54}=M_{54}>0$ gives $H_{54}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{58}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign58} gives $Q_{58}(-H_{58})=M_{58}$, and $M_{58}$ and $E_{58}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{58}>0$ and $E_{58}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-4$, write $x=-4-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{58}$ and $E_{58}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{58}>0$ and $E_{58}>0$. It remains to consider $-3\leq x\leq0$. For $x=-3$ and $x=-1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 2 residues of $y$ modulo $2$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with such $x$. For $x=0$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{3}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 3 residues of $y$ modulo $3$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=-2$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{7}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 7 residues of $y$ modulo $7$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{58}>0$ and $E_{58}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign58b} gives $4Q_{58}\geq E_{58}>0$, and $Q_{58}(-H_{58})=M_{58}>0$ gives $(-H_{58})>0$. Hence $H_{58}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{62}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign62} gives $Q_{62}(-H_{62})=M_{62}$, and $M_{62}$ and $E_{62}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{62}>0$ and $E_{62}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-1$, write $x=-1-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{62}$ and $E_{62}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{62}>0$ and $E_{62}>0$. It remains to consider $x=0$. For $x=0$ we have $M_{62}>0$ and $E_{62}>0$. Hence $M_{62}>0$ and $E_{62}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign62b} gives $4Q_{62}\geq E_{62}>0$, and $Q_{62}(-H_{62})=M_{62}>0$ gives $(-H_{62})>0$. Hence $H_{62}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{66}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign66} gives $Q_{66}(-H_{66})=M_{66}$, and $M_{66}$ and $E_{66}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{66}>0$ and $E_{66}>0$. If $x\geq3$, write $x=3+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-2$, write $x=-2-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{66}$ and $E_{66}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{66}>0$ and $E_{66}>0$. It remains to consider $-1\leq x\leq2$. For $x=-1$ we have $M_{66}>0$ and $E_{66}>0$. For $x=1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 2 residues of $y$ modulo $2$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=0$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{3}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 3 residues of $y$ modulo $3$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=2$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{29}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 29 residues of $y$ modulo $29$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{66}>0$ and $E_{66}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign66b} gives $4Q_{66}\geq E_{66}>0$, and $Q_{66}(-H_{66})=M_{66}>0$ gives $(-H_{66})>0$. Hence $H_{66}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{70}$.} Suppose that $H_{70}\leq0$, and put $Y=-H_{70}$, so that $Y\geq0$. Since $H_{70}=-x+y^{2}$, we have $x=y^{2}+Y$. If $y\geq0$, put $X=y$; if $y\leq-1$, put $X=-1-y$. In both cases $X\geq0$, and substituting into $F$ gives $F(x,y)=G_{70,1}(X,Y)$ in the first case and $F(x,y)=G_{70,2}(X,Y)$ in the second, where
\begin{align*}
G_{70,1}(X,Y)&=2X^{8}+8X^{6}Y+12X^{4}Y^{2}+2X^{3}+8X^{2}Y^{3}+XY+2X+2Y^{4}-1,\\
G_{70,2}(X,Y)&=2X^{8}+16X^{7}+8X^{6}Y+56X^{6}+48X^{5}Y+112X^{5}+12X^{4}Y^{2}\\
&\quad+120X^{4}Y+140X^{4}+48X^{3}Y^{2}+160X^{3}Y+110X^{3}\\
&\quad+8X^{2}Y^{3}+72X^{2}Y^{2}+120X^{2}Y+50X^{2}+16XY^{3}+48XY^{2}\\
&\quad+47XY+8X+2Y^{4}+8Y^{3}+12Y^{2}+7Y-3.
\end{align*}
Expanding $G_{70,1}(1+X,1+Y)$, and, for $i=0$, $G_{70,1}(i,1+Y)$ and $G_{70,1}(1+X,i)$, gives in each case coefficients of one sign and a non-zero constant term; moreover $G_{70,1}(i,j)\neq0$ for $i=j=0$. Hence $G_{70,1}(X,Y)\neq0$ for all $X,Y\geq0$. Expanding $G_{70,2}(1+X,1+Y)$, and, for $i=0$, $G_{70,2}(i,1+Y)$ and $G_{70,2}(1+X,i)$, gives in each case coefficients of one sign and a non-zero constant term; moreover $G_{70,2}(i,j)\neq0$ for $i=j=0$. Hence $G_{70,2}(X,Y)\neq0$ for all $X,Y\geq0$. In both cases $F(x,y)\neq0$, a contradiction. Hence $H_{70}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{73}$.} Suppose that $H_{73}\leq0$, and put $Y=-H_{73}$, so that $Y\geq0$. Since $H_{73}=x+y^{2}-y$, we have $x=-y^{2}+y-Y$. If $y\geq0$, put $X=y$; if $y\leq-1$, put $X=-1-y$. In both cases $X\geq0$, and substituting into $F$ gives $F(x,y)=G_{73,1}(X,Y)$ in the first case and $F(x,y)=G_{73,2}(X,Y)$ in the second, where
\begin{align*}
G_{73,1}(X,Y)&=2X^{8}-8X^{7}+8X^{6}Y+12X^{6}-24X^{5}Y-8X^{5}+12X^{4}Y^{2}\\
&\quad+24X^{4}Y+2X^{4}-24X^{3}Y^{2}-8X^{3}Y+8X^{2}Y^{3}+12X^{2}Y^{2}\\
&\quad+X^{2}-8XY^{3}-XY+2X+2Y^{4}-1,\\
G_{73,2}(X,Y)&=2X^{8}+24X^{7}+8X^{6}Y+124X^{6}+72X^{5}Y+360X^{5}+12X^{4}Y^{2}\\
&\quad+264X^{4}Y+642X^{4}+72X^{3}Y^{2}+504X^{3}Y+720X^{3}\\
&\quad+8X^{2}Y^{3}+156X^{2}Y^{2}+528X^{2}Y+497X^{2}+24XY^{3}\\
&\quad+144XY^{2}+289XY+192X+2Y^{4}+16Y^{3}+48Y^{2}+65Y+30.
\end{align*}
Expanding $G_{73,1}(1+X,1+Y)$, and, for $i=0$, $G_{73,1}(i,1+Y)$ and $G_{73,1}(1+X,i)$, gives in each case coefficients of one sign and a non-zero constant term; moreover $G_{73,1}(i,j)\neq0$ for $i=j=0$. Hence $G_{73,1}(X,Y)\neq0$ for all $X,Y\geq0$. All coefficients of $G_{73,2}$ have the same sign and its constant term is non-zero, so $G_{73,2}(X,Y)\neq0$ for all $X,Y\geq0$. In both cases $F(x,y)\neq0$, a contradiction. Hence $H_{73}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{74}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign74} gives $Q_{74}H_{74}=M_{74}$, and $M_{74}$ and $E_{74}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{74}>0$ and $E_{74}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-2$, write $x=-2-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{74}$ and $E_{74}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{74}>0$ and $E_{74}>0$. It remains to consider $-1\leq x\leq0$. For $x=-1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 2 residues of $y$ modulo $2$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=0$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{3}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 3 residues of $y$ modulo $3$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{74}>0$ and $E_{74}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign74b} gives $4Q_{74}\geq E_{74}>0$, and $Q_{74}H_{74}=M_{74}>0$ gives $H_{74}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{75}$.} Suppose that $(-H_{75})\leq0$, and put $Y=H_{75}$, so that $Y\geq0$. Since $(-H_{75})=-x+y^{2}-1$, we have $x=y^{2}+Y-1$. If $y\geq0$, put $X=y$; if $y\leq-1$, put $X=-1-y$. In both cases $X\geq0$, and substituting into $F$ gives $F(x,y)=G_{75,1}(X,Y)$ in the first case and $F(x,y)=G_{75,2}(X,Y)$ in the second, where
\begin{align*}
G_{75,1}(X,Y)&=2X^{8}+8X^{6}Y-8X^{6}+12X^{4}Y^{2}-24X^{4}Y+12X^{4}+2X^{3}\\
&\quad+8X^{2}Y^{3}-24X^{2}Y^{2}+24X^{2}Y-8X^{2}+XY+X+2Y^{4}-8Y^{3}\\
&\quad+12Y^{2}-8Y+1,\\
G_{75,2}(X,Y)&=2X^{8}+16X^{7}+8X^{6}Y+48X^{6}+48X^{5}Y+64X^{5}+12X^{4}Y^{2}\\
&\quad+96X^{4}Y+32X^{4}+48X^{3}Y^{2}+64X^{3}Y-2X^{3}+8X^{2}Y^{3}\\
&\quad+48X^{2}Y^{2}-6X^{2}+16XY^{3}-XY-7X+2Y^{4}-Y-4.
\end{align*}
Expanding $G_{75,1}(2+X,2+Y)$, and, for $0\leq i<2$, $G_{75,1}(i,2+Y)$ and $G_{75,1}(2+X,i)$, gives in each case coefficients of one sign and a non-zero constant term; moreover $G_{75,1}(i,j)\neq0$ for $0\leq i,j<2$. Hence $G_{75,1}(X,Y)\neq0$ for all $X,Y\geq0$. Expanding $G_{75,2}(2+X,2+Y)$, and, for $0\leq i<2$, $G_{75,2}(i,2+Y)$ and $G_{75,2}(2+X,i)$, gives in each case coefficients of one sign and a non-zero constant term; moreover $G_{75,2}(i,j)\neq0$ for $0\leq i,j<2$. Hence $G_{75,2}(X,Y)\neq0$ for all $X,Y\geq0$. In both cases $F(x,y)\neq0$, a contradiction. Hence $(-H_{75})>0$. Hence $H_{75}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{76}$.} Suppose that $H_{76}\leq0$. Then \eqref{eq:sign76} gives $(y)(-2x+y-2)<0$, so the two factors have opposite signs. As these factors are integers, this means that $y\geq1$ and $-2x+y\leq1$, or $y\leq-1$ and $-2x+y\geq3$. In the first case, write $y=X+1$ and $-2x+y=-Y+1$ with integers $X,Y\geq0$, so that $x=\tfrac{X+Y}{2}$ and $y=X+1$. Then $8F(x,y)=G_{76,1}(X,Y)$, where
\begin{align*}
G_{76,1}(X,Y)&=X^{4}+4X^{3}Y+8X^{3}+6X^{2}Y^{2}+28X^{2}+4XY^{3}+4XY+44X+Y^{4}\\
&\quad+4Y+16.
\end{align*}
All coefficients of $G_{76,1}$ have the same sign and its constant term is non-zero, so $G_{76,1}(X,Y)\neq0$ for all $X,Y\geq0$. In the second case, write $y=-X-1$ and $-2x+y=Y+3$ with integers $X,Y\geq0$, so that $x=\tfrac{-X-Y-4}{2}$ and $y=-X-1$. Then $8F(x,y)=G_{76,2}(X,Y)$, where
\begin{align*}
G_{76,2}(X,Y)&=X^{4}+4X^{3}Y+8X^{3}+6X^{2}Y^{2}+48X^{2}Y+76X^{2}+4XY^{3}\\
&\quad+48XY^{2}+196XY+236X+Y^{4}+16Y^{3}+96Y^{2}+260Y+240.
\end{align*}
All coefficients of $G_{76,2}$ have the same sign and its constant term is non-zero, so $G_{76,2}(X,Y)\neq0$ for all $X,Y\geq0$. In both cases $F(x,y)\neq0$, a contradiction. Hence $H_{76}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{78}$.} If $H_{78}\leq0$, then \eqref{eq:sign78} gives $(x+2y+3)^2\leq 0$ and $3(x+1)^2\leq 0$, so $|x+2y+3|\leq0$ and $|x+1|\leq0$. Solving these two linear forms for $x$ and $y$, we find that the only integer point satisfying both inequalities is $(-1,-1)$, and it is not a solution of \eqref{eq:main} with $H_{78}\leq0$. Hence $H_{78}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{83}$.} Suppose that $(-H_{83})\leq0$. Then \eqref{eq:sign83} gives $(x+y-2)(y+2)<0$, so the two factors have opposite signs. As these factors are integers, this means that $x+y\geq3$ and $y\leq-3$, or $x+y\leq1$ and $y\geq-1$. In the first case, write $x+y=3+X$ and $y=-3-Y$ with $X,Y\geq0$, so that $x=X+Y+6$ and $y=-Y-3$. Then $F(x,y)=G_{83,1}(X,Y)$, where
\begin{align*}
G_{83,1}(X,Y)&=2X^{4}+8X^{3}Y+48X^{3}+12X^{2}Y^{2}+144X^{2}Y+432X^{2}+8XY^{3}\\
&\quad+144XY^{2}+863XY+1725X+2Y^{4}+47Y^{3}+422Y^{2}+1690Y+2540.
\end{align*}
All coefficients of $G_{83,1}$ have the same sign and its constant term is non-zero, so $G_{83,1}(X,Y)\neq0$. In the second case, write $x+y=1-X$ and $y=-1+Y$ with $X,Y\geq0$, so that $x=-X-Y+2$ and $y=Y-1$. Then $F(x,y)=G_{83,2}(X,Y)$, where
\begin{align*}
G_{83,2}(X,Y)&=2X^{4}+8X^{3}Y-16X^{3}+12X^{2}Y^{2}-48X^{2}Y+48X^{2}+8XY^{3}\\
&\quad-48XY^{2}+95XY-63X+2Y^{4}-15Y^{3}+44Y^{2}-56Y+26.
\end{align*}
Expanding $G_{83,2}(4+X,4+Y)$, and, for $0\leq i<4$, $G_{83,2}(i,4+Y)$ and $G_{83,2}(4+X,i)$, gives in each case coefficients of one sign and a non-zero constant term; moreover $G_{83,2}(i,j)\neq0$ for $0\leq i,j<4$. Hence $G_{83,2}(X,Y)\neq0$ for all $X,Y\geq0$. In both cases $F(x,y)\neq0$, a contradiction. Hence $(-H_{83})>0$. Hence $H_{83}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{84}$.} Suppose that $(-H_{84})\leq0$. Since $(-H_{84})=-y-1$, this means $y\geq-1$. By \eqref{eq:sign84}, $F$ is $2x^4+L_{84}x+Q_{84}$, where $L_{84}$ and $Q_{84}$ depend only on $y$. If $y\geq1$, write $y=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; expanding $Q_{84}$ and $\Delta_{84}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $Q_{84}>0$ and $\Delta_{84}>0$. For $y=-1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{11}$ for every $x$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $y$. For $y=0$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $x$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $y$. Hence $Q_{84}>0$ and $\Delta_{84}>0$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:amgm} with $a=2$, $b=L_{84}$, $c=Q_{84}$ and $t=x$ gives $F(x,y)>0$, a contradiction. Hence $(-H_{84})>0$. Hence $H_{84}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{87}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign87} gives $Q_{87}H_{87}=M_{87}$, and $M_{87}$ and $E_{87}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{87}>0$ and $E_{87}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-3$, write $x=-3-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{87}$ and $E_{87}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{87}>0$ and $E_{87}>0$. It remains to consider $-2\leq x\leq0$. For $x=-1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 2 residues of $y$ modulo $2$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=0$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{3}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 3 residues of $y$ modulo $3$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=-2$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{7}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 7 residues of $y$ modulo $7$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{87}>0$ and $E_{87}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign87b} gives $64Q_{87}\geq E_{87}>0$, and $Q_{87}H_{87}=M_{87}>0$ gives $H_{87}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{89}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign89} gives $Q_{89}H_{89}=M_{89}$, and $M_{89}$ and $E_{89}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{89}>0$ and $E_{89}>0$. If $x\geq3$, write $x=3+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-2$, write $x=-2-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{89}$ and $E_{89}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{89}>0$ and $E_{89}>0$. It remains to consider $-1\leq x\leq2$. For $x=-1$ we have $M_{89}>0$ and $E_{89}>0$. For $x=1$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 2 residues of $y$ modulo $2$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=0$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{3}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 3 residues of $y$ modulo $3$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=2$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{29}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 29 residues of $y$ modulo $29$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{89}>0$ and $E_{89}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign89b} gives $4Q_{89}\geq E_{89}>0$, and $Q_{89}H_{89}=M_{89}>0$ gives $H_{89}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{91}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign91} gives $Q_{91}(-H_{91})=M_{91}$, and $M_{91}$ and $E_{91}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{91}>0$ and $E_{91}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-2$, write $x=-2-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{91}$ and $E_{91}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{91}>0$ and $E_{91}>0$. It remains to consider $-1\leq x\leq0$. For $x=-1$ and $x=0$ we have $M_{91}>0$ and $E_{91}>0$. Hence $M_{91}>0$ and $E_{91}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign91b} gives $4Q_{91}\geq E_{91}>0$, and $Q_{91}(-H_{91})=M_{91}>0$ gives $(-H_{91})>0$. Hence $H_{91}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{99}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign99} gives $Q_{99}H_{99}=M_{99}$, and $M_{99}$ and $E_{99}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{99}>0$ and $E_{99}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-2$, write $x=-2-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{99}$ and $E_{99}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{99}>0$ and $E_{99}>0$. It remains to consider $-1\leq x\leq0$. For $x=-1$ and $x=0$ we have $M_{99}>0$ and $E_{99}>0$. Hence $M_{99}>0$ and $E_{99}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign99b} gives $4Q_{99}\geq E_{99}>0$, and $Q_{99}H_{99}=M_{99}>0$ gives $H_{99}>0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{101}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign101} gives $Q_{101}(-H_{101})=M_{101}$, and $M_{101}$ and $E_{101}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{101}>0$ and $E_{101}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-2$, write $x=-2-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{101}$ and $E_{101}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{101}>0$ and $E_{101}>0$. It remains to consider $-1\leq x\leq0$. For $x=-1$ and $x=0$ we have $M_{101}>0$ and $E_{101}>0$. Hence $M_{101}>0$ and $E_{101}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign101b} gives $64Q_{101}\geq E_{101}>0$, and $Q_{101}(-H_{101})=M_{101}>0$ gives $(-H_{101})>0$. Hence $H_{101}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{102}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign102} gives $Q_{102}(-H_{102})=M_{102}$, and $M_{102}$ and $E_{102}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{102}>0$ and $E_{102}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-1$, write $x=-1-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{102}$ and $E_{102}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{102}>0$ and $E_{102}>0$. It remains to consider $x=0$. For $x=0$ we have $M_{102}>0$ and $E_{102}>0$. Hence $M_{102}>0$ and $E_{102}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign102b} gives $4Q_{102}\geq E_{102}>0$, and $Q_{102}(-H_{102})=M_{102}>0$ gives $(-H_{102})>0$. Hence $H_{102}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{104}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign104} gives $Q_{104}(-H_{104})=M_{104}$, and $M_{104}$ and $E_{104}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{104}>0$ and $E_{104}>0$. If $x\geq3$, write $x=3+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-4$, write $x=-4-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{104}$ and $E_{104}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{104}>0$ and $E_{104}>0$. It remains to consider $-3\leq x\leq2$. For $x\in\{-3,\allowbreak -1,\allowbreak 1\}$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 2 residues of $y$ modulo $2$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with such $x$. For $x=0$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{3}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 3 residues of $y$ modulo $3$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=-2$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{7}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 7 residues of $y$ modulo $7$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=2$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{29}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 29 residues of $y$ modulo $29$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{104}>0$ and $E_{104}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign104b} gives $4Q_{104}\geq E_{104}>0$, and $Q_{104}(-H_{104})=M_{104}>0$ gives $(-H_{104})>0$. Hence $H_{104}<0$.

\emph{The sign of $H_{105}$.} Since $F(x,y)=0$, \eqref{eq:sign105} gives $Q_{105}H_{105}=M_{105}$, and $M_{105}$ and $E_{105}$ depend only on $x$. We show that $M_{105}>0$ and $E_{105}>0$. If $x\geq1$, write $x=1+z$ with $z\geq0$; if $x\leq-4$, write $x=-4-z$ with $z\geq0$. In both cases, expanding $M_{105}$ and $E_{105}$ as polynomials in $z$ gives non-negative coefficients and positive constant terms, so $M_{105}>0$ and $E_{105}>0$. It remains to consider $-3\leq x\leq0$. For $x=-1$ and $x=0$ we have $M_{105}>0$ and $E_{105}>0$. For $x=-3$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{2}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 2 residues of $y$ modulo $2$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. For $x=-2$, we have $F(x,y)\not\equiv0\pmod{7}$ for every $y$, as one checks for the 7 residues of $y$ modulo $7$, so \eqref{eq:main} has no solution with this $x$. Hence $M_{105}>0$ and $E_{105}>0$, so \eqref{eq:sign105b} gives $4Q_{105}\geq E_{105}>0$, and $Q_{105}H_{105}=M_{105}>0$ gives $H_{105}>0$.

For a place $v$, put $B_v=\prod_{\{i,j\}}(H_i,H_j)_v$, the product over the edges. By Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(i), $(H_i,H_j)_\infty=-1$ exactly when $H_i<0$ and $H_j<0$. Here $H_i>0$ for $i\in\{0,\allowbreak 1,\allowbreak 2,\allowbreak 3,\allowbreak 4,\allowbreak 5,\allowbreak 6,\allowbreak 7,\allowbreak 8,\allowbreak 9,\allowbreak 10,\allowbreak 11,\allowbreak 12,\allowbreak 13,\allowbreak 14,\allowbreak 28,\allowbreak 31,\allowbreak 32,\allowbreak 33,\allowbreak 35,\allowbreak 36,\allowbreak 40,\allowbreak 43,\allowbreak 50,\allowbreak 54,\allowbreak 70,\allowbreak 73,\allowbreak 74,\allowbreak 76,\allowbreak 78,\allowbreak 86,\allowbreak 87,\allowbreak 88,\allowbreak 89,\allowbreak 90,\allowbreak 94,\allowbreak 97,\allowbreak 99,\allowbreak 105,\allowbreak 106\}$ and $H_i<0$ for $i\in\{18,\allowbreak 19,\allowbreak 22,\allowbreak 27,\allowbreak 58,\allowbreak 62,\allowbreak 66,\allowbreak 75,\allowbreak 83,\allowbreak 84,\allowbreak 91,\allowbreak 95,\allowbreak 101,\allowbreak 102,\allowbreak 104\}$. For $i\in\{15,\allowbreak 16,\allowbreak 17,\allowbreak 20,\allowbreak 21,\allowbreak 23,\allowbreak 24,\allowbreak 25,\allowbreak 26,\allowbreak 29,\allowbreak 30,\allowbreak 34,\allowbreak 37,\allowbreak 38,\allowbreak 39,\allowbreak 41,\allowbreak 42,\allowbreak 44,\allowbreak 45,\allowbreak 46,\allowbreak 47,\allowbreak 48,\allowbreak 49,\allowbreak 51,\allowbreak 52,\allowbreak 53,\allowbreak 55,\allowbreak 56,\allowbreak 57,\allowbreak 59,\allowbreak 60,\allowbreak 61,\allowbreak 63,\allowbreak 64,\allowbreak 65,\allowbreak 67,\allowbreak 68,\allowbreak 69,\allowbreak 71,\allowbreak 72,\allowbreak 77,\allowbreak 79,\allowbreak 80,\allowbreak 81,\allowbreak 82,\allowbreak 85,\allowbreak 92,\allowbreak 93,\allowbreak 96,\allowbreak 98,\allowbreak 100,\allowbreak 103\}$, the sign of $H_i$ is not needed: $H_j>0$ for every edge $\{i,j\}$, so $(H_i,H_j)_\infty=1$. Counting the edges whose two values are negative, we find $B_\infty=1$.

\paragraph{Step 3: the quadratic graphs at the odd primes outside $S$.} Let $S=\{2,\allowbreak 3,\allowbreak 5,\allowbreak 7,\allowbreak 13\}$. It contains $2$ and the primes dividing the $m_{i}$, the $k_{ij}$ and the constant vertices. Let $p\notin S$ be an odd prime; we show that $B_p=1$. For an edge $\{i,j\}$ such that $p$ divides neither $H_i$ nor $H_j$, $(H_i,H_j)_p=1$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(ii). Now let $i$ be a vertex with $p\mid H_i$. Then $H_i$ is not constant, as $p\notin S$. For every edge $\{i,j\}$, $p\nmid H_j$: if $H_j$ is constant, because $p\notin S$, and otherwise by \eqref{eq:sep}, because $p\nmid k_{ij}$. In particular, no edge has both values divisible by $p$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(v), the edges at $i$ contribute $\prod_{j\sim i}(H_i,H_j)_p=(H_i,\Pi_i)_p$, where $\Pi_i=\prod_{j\sim i}H_j$ is not divisible by $p$. By \eqref{eq:star}, $m_i^2\Pi_i\equiv W_i^2\pmod p$, and $p\nmid m_i$, so $\Pi_i$ is a non-zero square modulo $p$, and $(H_i,\Pi_i)_p=(\Pi_i,H_i)_p=1$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:square}. Hence $B_p=1$.

\paragraph{Step 4: cubic reciprocity.} For every place $v$ of $K$, put
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cB}
\mathcal B_v=\sum_{(i,j)}w_{ij}\,\theta_v(J_i,J_j)+\sum_i\theta_v(J_i,c_i)\in\Z/3\Z,
\end{equation}
the first sum over the edges, and, for a rational prime $p$, put $\mathcal B_p=\sum_{v\mid p}\mathcal B_v$. Applying (H3) to every pair $(J_i,J_j)$ with an edge, multiplied by $w_{ij}$, and to every pair $(J_i,c_i)$, and adding, we find that $\mathcal B_v=0$ for all but finitely many $v$ and $\sum_pB_p=0$, as the complex place contributes $0$. By (H1) and the definition of $\mathrm{cl}_p$, $\mathcal B_p=\sum_{(i,j)}w_{ij}\theta_p(\mathrm{cl}_p(J_i),\mathrm{cl}_p(J_j))+\sum_i\theta_p(\mathrm{cl}_p(J_i),\mathrm{cl}_p(c_i))$, where $\theta_p$ is the form of Lemma~\ref{lem:cM3} if $p=3$.

\paragraph{Step 5: the cubic certificates at the primes outside $S$.} The set $S$ also contains $3$ and the primes dividing the norms of the constants $c_i$, of the $D_i$, of the $R_{ij}$ and of the $D_{ij}$. Let $p\notin S$, and let $v$ be a place above $p$, with residue field $k_v$; then all these numbers are units at $v$. First, no edge $(i,j)$ has both values divisible by $v$. Indeed, suppose that $v$ divides $J_i$ and $J_j$. If $R_{ij}$ is defined, let $k$ be the linear endpoint used for it and $\{k,l\}=\{i,j\}$, and put $t=\xi$. As $J_k=\varepsilon_k(\eta-r_k(\xi))$, we have $\eta\equiv r_k(t)$ modulo $v$, hence $f_k(t)\equiv F(x,y)=0$ and $g_{kl}(t)\equiv J_l\equiv0$ modulo $v$. The resultant can be written as $R_{ij}=Af_k+Cg_{kl}$ with polynomials $A$ and $C$ with coefficients in $\Z[\omega]$, so $v$ divides $R_{ij}$, a contradiction. Otherwise, \eqref{eq:csep} shows that $v$ divides $D_{ij}$, again a contradiction. By (H2), a term of \eqref{eq:cB} in which all arguments are units vanishes. Now let $v$ divide $J_i$. Collecting the terms of \eqref{eq:cB} which contain $J_i$, and using (H1) and $\rho_{ji}=-w_{ij}$ for the edges $(j,i)$ with $j<i$, we obtain $\theta_v(J_i,\Pi_i)$ with $\Pi_i=c_i\prod_jJ_j^{\rho_{ij}}$, which is a unit. We claim that $D_i^3\Pi_i\equiv W^3$ modulo $v$ for some $W\in\Z[\omega]$. If $i$ is linear, put $t=\xi$; then $g_{ij}(t)\equiv J_j$ and $f_i(t)\equiv0$ modulo $v$ as above, and \eqref{eq:cstarlin} gives the claim with $W=w_i(t)$. Otherwise, \eqref{eq:cstar} gives the claim with $W=T_i(x,y)$. Hence the reduction of $\Pi_i$ is a non-zero cube in $k_v$, $\chi(\Pi_i)=0$, and $\theta_v(J_i,\Pi_i)=v(J_i)\chi(\Pi_i)=0$ by (H2). Hence $\mathcal B_v=0$, and $\mathcal B_p=0$.

\paragraph{Step 6: the primes in $S$.} Let $p\in S$, and suppose that $x\equiv a$ and $y\equiv b\pmod{p^k}$. Lemma~\ref{lem:residue} gives, for every vertex $H_i$ of the quadratic graph, a vector $b_i$ and a subspace $D_i$ with $c_p(H_i)\in b_i+D_i$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:cresidue} gives the analogous data for the values $J_i$ of the cubic certificate. If the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:envelope} hold for every quadratic graph and those of Lemma~\ref{lem:cenvelope} hold for every cubic certificate, then $B_p$ and $\mathcal B_p$ are determined by $a$, $b$ and $k$. Starting from the solutions of \eqref{eq:main} modulo $p$, we refine a residue class $x\equiv a$, $y\equiv b\pmod{p^k}$ to the classes modulo $p^{k+1}$ that contain solutions of \eqref{eq:main} modulo $p^{k+1}$, until all these hypotheses hold, or until no solution remains. Table~\ref{tab:local} lists the resulting terminal classes together with the joint value $(\mathcal B_p;B_p)$; every integer solution lies in one of them. The possible joint values are: for $p=2$, $(0;-1)$; for $p=3$, $(1;1),\allowbreak (2;-1)$; for $p=5$, $(0;1)$; for $p=7$, $(2;1)$; for $p=13$, $(0;1)$.

\paragraph{Step 7: contradiction.} By Steps~4 and~5, $\sum_{p\in S}\mathcal B_p=0$. By Theorem~\ref{thm:hilbert}(iv) and Step~3, $\prod_{p\in S}B_p=B_\infty=1$ by Step~2. But by Step~6, the pair $(\textstyle\sum_{p\in S}\mathcal B_p;\prod_{p\in S}B_p)$ lies in the set consisting of the pairs $(0;-1)$ and $(1;1)$, which does not contain $(0;1)$. This contradiction proves the theorem.
\qed
